Each forward pass produces a stochastic action sample, so querying $\\pi_\\theta$ independently $K$ times yields $K$ candidate action chunks. Our goal is to produce a single action more accurate than any individual sample, with no changes to~$\\pi_\\theta$. The method applies to flow-matching VLAs that produce diverse action samples (Section~\\ref{sec:applicability})."
}
```

</details>

70	% exp_id: magnitude sweep obs_only seeds 42-46, perdof seeds 42-46
71	
72	The necessity of magnitude control connects ACR-VLA to the residual policy learning literature. Silver et al.~\cite{silver2018residual} introduced residual learning with zero-initialization for combining learned and classical controllers. TRANSIC~\cite{jiang2024transic} applies learned binary gating to selectively enable residual actions. PLD~\cite{xiao2026pld} bounds corrections within a fixed range during RL training. ACR-VLA uses fixed $L_2$ norm clipping, the simplest instantiation: a hard constraint that prevents the correction from overwhelming the base policy, with no learned gating or multi-stage pipeline.
73	
74	\subsection{Inference Protocol}

{

  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_8/projects/acr-vla-conformal-refinement/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "A2C2 is the closest concurrent work to ACR-VLA: both attach a lightweight correction module to a frozen VLA without retraining the base policy. The key differences are threefold.\nFirst, ACR-VLA operates as a two-stage pipeline, where multi-sample selection (choosing the candidate closest to the sample mean by $L_2$ distance) precedes bounded residual correction, whereas A2C2 applies per-step correction directly without a selection stage.\nSecond, ACR-VLA enforces explicit magnitude control via $L_2$ norm clipping, which our ablations show is critical for preventing compounding correction errors (Section~\\ref{sec:magnitude_results}).\nThird, ACR-VLA conditions the correction module on the frozen VLA's observation embedding rather than temporal action features.",
  "new_string": "A2C2 is the closest concurrent work to ours: both attach a lightweight correction module to a frozen VLA without retraining the base policy. The key differences are threefold.\nFirst, our pipeline operates as a two-stage process, where multi-sample selection (choosing the candidate closest to the sample mean by $L_2$ distance) precedes bounded residual correction, whereas A2C2 applies per-step correction directly without a selection stage.\nSecond, our pipeline enforces explicit magnitude control via $L_2$ norm clipping, which our ablations show is critical for preventing compounding correction errors (Section~\\ref{sec:magnitude_results}).\nThird, our pipeline conditions the correction module on the frozen VLA's observation embedding rather than temporal action features."
}
```

### Tool result

_source line 1984 · 2026-05-18T10:49:05.480532+00:00_

28	We identify seven limitations, each of which qualifies the scope of our findings:
29	\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
30	  \item \emph{Simulation only.} All experiments use LIBERO~\cite{liu2023libero}; real-robot transfer is untested.
31	  \item \emph{Single VLA backbone.} We evaluate only SmolVLA~\cite{shukor2025smolvla} (450M parameters). Whether ACR-VLA transfers to larger VLAs (e.g., $\pi_0$, OpenVLA) is unknown.
32	  \item \emph{Limited benchmarks.} Two LIBERO suites (20 tasks total); other manipulation benchmarks and perturbation types are not evaluated.
33	  % exp_id: obs_only per-task variance — some object tasks underperform argmin
34	  \item \emph{Per-task variance.} On individual LIBERO-Object tasks, obs\_only occasionally underperforms the Argmin baseline. The method does not universally help.
35	  \item \emph{Magnitude bound requires tuning.} We use $\varepsilon{=}0.2$ throughout, but the optimal $\varepsilon$ likely varies across VLA architectures and task domains.
36	  % exp_id: obs_only eps0.2 seeds 42-46: 22/16/16/16/15%, mean 17.0±2.8%
37	  \item \emph{Seed variance.} Across six seeds (42--47), obs\_only ($\varepsilon{=}0.2$) ranges from 15\% to 22\% (mean $17.5 \pm 2.8$\%). All seeds exceed the VLA-only mean (11.2\%), but per-seed variation is substantial.
38	  % exp_id: obs_only_eps0.1_object → 19.0%
39	  \item \emph{Absolute performance.} 19.0\% success on LIBERO-Object is far from deployment-ready. ACR-VLA reduces the gap relative to selection-only but does not close it.
40	\end{enumerate}
41	
42

_source line 1990 · 2026-05-18T10:49:19.359866+00:00_

44	\label{sec:conclusion}
45	
46	% exp_id: obs_only eps=0.2 5-seed mean 17.0±2.8%, VLA-only 4-seed mean 10.75±1.5%
47	% exp_id: obs_only_eps0.1_spatial → 35.0%, argmin_K10_spatial → 34.0%
48	We presented ACR-VLA, a sample-then-correct pipeline that wraps a frozen flow-matching VLA with $L_2$ argmin selection followed by a lightweight residual correction module (${\sim}$327K parameters, ${\sim}$0.3\,ms overhead).
49	On LIBERO-Object, the full pipeline significantly improves over VLA-only (6-seed mean $17.5 \pm 2.8$\% vs.\ $11.2 \pm 1.6$\%, $p = 0.0074$).
50	The margin over selection alone (+5.1\,pp) is consistent across seeds.
51	On LIBERO-Spatial, correction provides no measurable benefit over selection (best CRM within 1\,pp of the selection baseline), indicating the approach is effective for fine-grained motor correction under object-level distribution shift but not for spatial re-planning.
52	
53	% exp_id: perdof_eps0.1_object → 11.0%, obs_only_eps0.1_object → 19.0%

**模板规格：**
- `\usepackage{corl_2026}`（review 模式自动匿名+行号）
- 8 页正文限制（initial submission），references/appendix 不计入
- 引用格式：natbib + corlabbrvnat.bst（按出现顺序编号 [1][2]...）
- **必须有 Limitations section**（计入 8 页）
- **必须有 Keywords**（Abstract 后）

**DDL 更正：2026-05-29**（之前误为 May 26）

**任务：**
1. 创建主 .tex 文件，使用 corl_2026.sty，引入各章节（\input）
2. 确保 8 页限制内（当前 article 模板 15 页，需要大幅压缩或拆分到 appendix）
3. 添加 Limitations section（Discussion 中已有部分内容可提取）
4. 添加 Keywords
5. 切换引用格式到 corlabbrvnat.bst
6. 编译验证

这个任务优先级高于 Step 5 的后续 Reviewer 轮次——先确保模板正确编译，再继续内容迭代。

3	% Latex templates for the Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL)
4	%
5	% This template is heavily inspired by the NeurIPS, ICML, ICLR and IEEE Transactions latex templates.
6	% Hence we would like to thank: Roman Garnett and the previous mantainers of the NIPS style, Percy Liang and the previous mantainers of the ICML style, Hugo Larochelle for the ICLR style, and Michael Shell for the IEEE Transactions style.

185	\subsection{Inference-Time Scaling with $K$}
186	\label{sec:k_scaling}
187	
188	Figure~\ref{fig:kvalue} plots task success rate against the number of VLA samples $K$.
189	
190	\begin{figure}[t]
191	\centering
192	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/fig3_kvalue.pdf}
193	\caption{Success rate (\%) vs.\ number of VLA samples $K$ on LIBERO-Object (obs\_only, $\varepsilon{=}0.2$). Reference lines: VLA-only (11.2\%, 5 seeds) and Argmin-only (12.4\%, 5 seeds). Error bars: $\pm$1 std where available; hollow marker for $K{=}5$ ($n{=}1$).}
194	\label{fig:kvalue}
195	\end{figure}
196	
197	Performance increases monotonically with $K$: $K{=}1$ (10.3\%, 3 seeds) $\to$ $K{=}5$ (14.0\%, 1 seed) $\to$ $K{=}10$ (17.5\%, 6 seeds).
198	At $K{=}1$, CRM correction alone performs at the VLA-only level (11.2\%), confirming that the correction module requires multi-sample selection to be effective.
199	The monotonic trend parallels inference-time compute scaling in LLMs~\cite{snell2024scaling}, where repeated sampling with selection (best-of-$N$) improves output quality.
200	In our setting, more samples yield a better argmin starting point, which in turn enables more effective correction.
201	
202	The $K{=}5$ data point is preliminary ($n{=}1$), but it falls on the monotonic trend, suggesting that even moderate sample budgets can capture partial gains.
203	The compute cost scales linearly: $K{=}10$ requires 10 forward passes through the frozen VLA plus ${\sim}$0.3\,ms for CRM inference, a ${\sim}$10$\times$ increase in VLA compute.
204	
205	
206	\subsection{Applicability Boundary: Stochastic VLA Diversity}
207	\label{sec:applicability}
208	
209	The multi-sample pipeline requires stochastic action diversity across samples. We test this prerequisite by comparing SmolVLA (flow-matching, 450M) with OpenVLA~\cite{kim2024openvla} (autoregressive tokenization, 7B).

212	\centering

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_8/projects/acr-vla-conformal-refinement/docs/paper/appendix.tex",
  "content": "\n\\appendix\n\n\\section{Inference-Time Scaling with $K$}\n\\label{sec:k_scaling}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:kvalue} plots task success rate against the number of VLA samples $K$.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/fig3_kvalue.pdf}\n\\caption{Success rate (\\%) vs.\\ number of VLA samples $K$ on LIBERO-Object (obs\\_only, $\\varepsilon{=}0.2$). Reference lines: VLA-only (11.2\\%, 5 seeds) and Argmin-only (12.4\\%, 5 seeds). Error bars: $\\pm$1 std where available; hollow marker for $K{=}5$ ($n{=}1$).}\n\\label{fig:kvalue}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPerformance increases monotonically with $K$: $K{=}1$ (10.3\\%, 3 seeds) $\\to$ $K{=}5$ (14.0\\%, 1 seed) $\\to$ $K{=}10$ (17.5\\%, 6 seeds).\nAt $K{=}1$, CRM correction alone performs at the VLA-only level (11.2\\%), confirming that the correction module requires multi-sample selection to be effective.\nThe monotonic trend parallels inference-time compute scaling in LLMs~\\cite{snell2024scaling}, where repeated sampling with selection (best-of-$N$) improves output quality.\nIn our setting, more samples yield a better argmin starting point, which in turn enables more effective correction.\n\nThe $K{=}5$ data point is preliminary ($n{=}1$), but it falls on the monotonic trend, suggesting that even moderate sample budgets can capture partial gains.\nThe compute cost scales linearly: $K{=}10$ requires 10 forward passes through the frozen VLA plus ${\\sim}$0.3\\,ms for CRM inference, a ${\\sim}$10$\\times$ increase in VLA compute.\n\n\n\\section{Applicability Boundary: Stochastic VLA Diversity}\n\\label{sec:applicability}\n\nThe multi-sample pipeline requires stochastic action diversity across samples. We test this prerequisite by comparing SmolVLA (flow-matching, 450M) with OpenVLA~\\cite{kim2024openvla} (autoregressive tokenization, 7B).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/fig4_diversity.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-DOF action variance (log scale) across $K{=}10$ samples for SmolVLA (flow-matching) and OpenVLA (tokenized). OpenVLA produces 8/10 identical samples at temperature $T{=}0.5$, with per-DOF variance below $10^{-30}$ for most dimensions.}\n\\label{fig:diversity}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:diversity} shows the contrast: SmolVLA produces meaningfully diverse samples across all action dimensions, while OpenVLA at temperature $T{=}0.5$ generates 8 out of 10 identical action sequences, with per-DOF variance below $10^{-30}$ for most dimensions.\nThis near-determinism arises from autoregressive token selection: the argmax decoding at each token position collapses the distribution to a single mode, and moderate temperature fails to overcome the peaked distribution.\n\nThis result establishes an applicability boundary: multi-sample inference-time correction is specific to stochastic generative VLAs (flow-matching, diffusion). Tokenized VLAs lack the action diversity that selection and correction rely on.\nThe boundary is architectural, not a limitation of our method: any inference-time approach that draws multiple action candidates requires the base policy to produce diverse samples.\n\n\n\\section{Per-Task Analysis}\n\\label{sec:per_task}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:pertask} reports per-task success rates on LIBERO-Object (seed 42) for the VLA-only baseline, Argmin selection, and obs\\_only at two magnitude bounds.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-task success rate (\\%) on LIBERO-Object (seed 42, 10 rollouts per task). Tasks ordered by obs\\_only $\\varepsilon{=}0.2$ improvement over VLA-only.}\n\\label{tab:pertask}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nTask & VLA-only & Argmin & obs\\_only 0.1 & obs\\_only 0.2 \\\\\n\\midrule\nchocolate\\_pudding & 20 & 30 & 50 & 60 \\\\\nketchup            &  0 &  0 & 10 & 20 \\\\\nbbq\\_sauce         & 20 & 30 & 40 & 40 \\\\\nsalad\\_dressing    & 20 & 10 & 20 & 30 \\\\\nbutter             &  0 & 10 & 10 & 10 \\\\\nmilk               & 10 & 10 & 20 & 20 \\\\\norange\\_juice      & 30 & 30 & 30 & 30 \\\\\ntomato\\_sauce      & 10 &  0 &  0 & 10 \\\\\nalphabet\\_soup     &  0 &  0 &  0 &  0 \\\\\ncream\\_cheese      & 10 &  0 & 10 &  0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nImprovement concentrates on medium-difficulty tasks.\nThe largest improvements appear on tasks where the frozen VLA partially succeeds but makes correctable errors: chocolate\\_pudding (+40\\,pp, from 20\\% to 60\\%), bbq\\_sauce and ketchup (+20\\,pp each), and salad\\_dressing (+10\\,pp).\nThese four tasks account for 9 of the 10 additional successes.\nConversely, four tasks (alphabet\\_soup, cream\\_cheese, tomato\\_sauce, butter) remain at 0--10\\% across all methods.\nFor these tasks, the base policy fails so completely that local action-level correction cannot recover a viable trajectory.\n\nCorrection benefits tasks where the frozen VLA partially succeeds but makes correctable errors, rather than tasks where the base policy fails catastrophically or already succeeds reliably.\nThis pattern is expected for a residual correction approach bounded by $\\varepsilon$: the CRM can fix small deviations in grasp pose or approach angle, but cannot recover from fundamental planning failures.\nThe concentrated improvement profile also addresses the question of effect size: the aggregate +10\\,pp is not a uniform +1\\,pp across 10 tasks, but a substantial +20--40\\,pp on specific tasks diluted by tasks outside the correctable regime.\n"
}

\\\\\nbutter             &  0 & 10 & 10 & 10 \\\\\nmilk               & 10 & 10 & 20 & 20 \\\\\norange\\_juice      & 30 & 30 & 30 & 30 \\\\\ntomato\\_sauce      & 10 &  0 &  0 & 10 \\\\\nalphabet\\_soup     &  0 &  0 &  0 &  0 \\\\\ncream\\_cheese      & 10 &  0 & 10 &  0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Improvement concentrates on medium-difficulty tasks.}\nThe +10\\,pp aggregate gain of obs\\_only ($\\varepsilon{=}0.2$) over VLA-only is not uniformly distributed across tasks.\nThe largest improvements appear on tasks where the frozen VLA partially succeeds but makes correctable errors: chocolate\\_pudding (+40\\,pp, from 20\\% to 60\\%), bbq\\_sauce and ketchup (+20\\,pp each), and salad\\_dressing (+10\\,pp).\nThese four tasks account for 9 of the 10 additional successes.\nConversely, four tasks (alphabet\\_soup, cream\\_cheese, tomato\\_sauce, butter) remain at 0--10\\% across all methods, including obs\\_only.\nFor these tasks, the base policy fails so completely that local action-level correction cannot recover a viable trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Implications for the correction mechanism.}\nCorrection benefits tasks where the frozen VLA partially succeeds but makes correctable errors, rather than tasks where the base policy fails catastrophically or already succeeds reliably.\nThis pattern is expected for a residual correction approach bounded by $\\varepsilon$: the CRM can fix small deviations in grasp pose or approach angle, but cannot recover from fundamental planning failures (e.g., reaching toward the wrong object entirely).\nThe concentrated improvement profile also addresses the question of effect size: the aggregate +10\\,pp is not a uniform +1\\,pp across 10 tasks, but a substantial +20--40\\,pp on specific tasks diluted by tasks outside the correctable regime.",
    "new_string": "\\paragraph{Inference-time scaling with $K$.}\nPerformance increases monotonically with the number of VLA samples: $K{=}1$ (10.3\\%, 3 seeds) $\\to$ $K{=}5$ (14.0\\%, 1 seed) $\\to$ $K{=}10$ (17.5\\%, 6 seeds), paralleling test-time compute scaling in LLMs~\\cite{snell2024scaling}. At $K{=}1$, the CRM performs at the VLA-only level, confirming that multi-sample selection is a prerequisite for effective correction. Full analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:k_scaling}.\n\n\\paragraph{Applicability boundary.}\nThe pipeline requires stochastic action diversity. OpenVLA~\\cite{kim2024openvla} (autoregressive tokenization, 7B) produces 8/10 identical samples at temperature $T{=}0.5$, with per-DOF variance below $10^{-30}$, compared to SmolVLA's meaningfully diverse flow-matching samples. Multi-sample correction is specific to stochastic generative VLAs; tokenized VLAs lack the required diversity (Appendix~\\ref{sec:applicability}, Figure~\\ref{fig:diversity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task analysis.}\nThe aggregate improvement concentrates on medium-difficulty tasks where the VLA partially succeeds but makes correctable errors (e.g., chocolate\\_pudding: 20\\%$\\to$60\\%). Tasks where the base policy fails completely (alphabet\\_soup: 0\\%) remain uncorrectable. See Appendix~\\ref{sec:per_task} for full per-task results."
  }
}

59	where $\mathbf{a}_{\mathrm{expert}}$ is the demonstration action. The training set comprises 26{,}737 precomputed samples from 10 demonstrations per task across 20 LIBERO tasks. For each demonstration step, we run $K{=}10$ forward passes through the frozen SmolVLA, apply argmin selection~(\ref{eq:argmin}) to obtain $a_{\mathrm{base}}$, and train the CRM to predict the residual that moves $a_{\mathrm{base}}$ toward $\mathbf{a}_{\mathrm{expert}}$. Data augmentation includes additive Gaussian noise on the mean-action vector ($\sigma \in [0.02, 0.1]$) and, for variants that receive uncertainty inputs, noise on those features ($\sigma \approx 0.006$). Both forms of augmentation improve generalization to the state distribution encountered during closed-loop evaluation, which diverges from the demonstration distribution due to compounding errors~\cite{ross2011dagger}.
60	
61	\subsection{Magnitude Control}
62	\label{sec:magnitude}
63	
64	The norm bound $\varepsilon$ in~(\ref{eq:crm}) controls how far the CRM can deviate from the selected action and is critical for stable closed-loop performance.
65	
66	Without clipping, the CRM's natural output has $\|\boldsymbol{\delta}\|_2 \approx 1.42$. At this magnitude, compounding error across the trajectory destroys task performance: the perdof variant achieves 0\% success on LIBERO-Spatial across three seeds. Each over-corrected action shifts the state distribution away from the CRM's training regime, and subsequent corrections compound the deviation. This failure is analogous to the compounding error problem in imitation learning~\cite{ross2011dagger} but manifests at inference time rather than during data collection.

Each over-corrected action shifts the state distribution away from the CRM's training regime, and subsequent corrections compound the deviation. This failure is analogous to the compounding error problem in imitation learning~\\cite{ross2011dagger} but manifests at inference time rather than during data collection.\n% exp_id: eval-acr-spatial-v4-default-r3 (uncapped norm~1.42, perdof 0%, 3 seeds)\n\nConstraining $\\varepsilon$ to $[0.05, 0.2]$ yields stable corrections. We use $\\varepsilon{=}0.2$ as the primary setting. The obs\\_only variant is robust across all tested bounds ($\\varepsilon \\in [0.03, \\text{uncapped}]$), maintaining 13.5--17.5\\% on LIBERO-Object across the tested range ($n{=}1$--$6$ per point). The perdof variant degrades progressively as $\\varepsilon$ increases, collapsing from 14\\% to 0\\% over the same range. The root cause is noisy covariance estimation at $K{=}10$ samples in $d{=}6$ dimensions: per-DOF directional signals are poorly estimated with only 10 samples, harmless when clipped to small magnitude but catastrophic when the bound is loose. Section~\\ref{sec:magnitude_results} presents the full sensitivity analysis.\n% exp_id: magnitude sweep obs_only seeds 42-46, perdof seeds 42-46\n\nThe necessity of magnitude control connects our method to the residual policy learning literature. Silver et al.~\\cite{silver2018residual} introduced residual learning with zero-initialization for combining learned and classical controllers. TRANSIC~\\cite{jiang2024transic} applies learned binary gating to selectively enable residual actions. PLD~\\cite{xiao2026pld} bounds corrections within a fixed range during RL training. Our pipeline uses fixed $L_2$ norm clipping, the simplest instantiation: a hard constraint that prevents the correction from overwhelming the base policy, with no learned gating or multi-stage pipeline.\n\n\\subsection{Inference Protocol}\n\\label{sec:inference_protocol}\n\nEach control step proceeds as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep]\n  \\item Draw $K{=}10$ action chunks from the frozen VLA $\\pi_\\theta$ and truncate to $H{=}5$ steps.\n  \\item Compute the sample mean $\\boldsymbol{\\mu}_h$ per horizon step and select $a_{\\mathrm{base}}$ via $L_2$ argmin~(\\ref{eq:argmin}).\n  \\item Extract the observation embedding $\\mathbf{z}_{\\mathrm{obs}}$ from the frozen VLA encoder.\n  \\item Compute $\\boldsymbol{\\delta} = \\mathrm{CRM}([\\mathbf{z}_{\\mathrm{obs}};\\, a_{\\mathrm{base}}])$ and clip to $\\|\\boldsymbol{\\delta}\\|_2 \\leq \\varepsilon$.\n  \\item Execute the first action of $\\mathbf{a}^* = a_{\\mathrm{base}} + \\mathrm{clip}(\\boldsymbol{\\delta})$.\n  \\item Observe new state and re-plan from step~1.\n\\end{enumerate}\nThe computational overhead is $K{=}10$ extra forward passes through the base VLA plus ${\\sim}$0.3\\,ms for CRM inference. The base VLA is never modified.",
  "new_string": "\\subsection{Magnitude Control and Inference}\n\\label{sec:magnitude}\n\nThe norm bound $\\varepsilon$ in~(\\ref{eq:crm}) is critical for stable closed-loop performance. Without clipping, the CRM's natural output norm (${\\approx}$1.42) causes compounding errors that destroy task performance (0\\% success for the perdof variant on LIBERO-Spatial). Constraining $\\varepsilon$ to $[0.05, 0.2]$ yields stable corrections; we use $\\varepsilon{=}0.2$ as the primary setting. Section~\\ref{sec:magnitude_results} presents the full sensitivity analysis across conditioning variants and magnitude bounds.\n\nAt each control step, the pipeline: (1) draws $K{=}10$ action chunks from the frozen VLA, (2) selects the closest sample to the mean via $L_2$ argmin, (3) applies the CRM with norm clipping, and (4) executes the first action of the corrected chunk before re-planning. The overhead is $K{=}10$ VLA forward passes plus ${\\sim}$0.3\\,ms for CRM inference."
}

1	
2	\section{Discussion}
3	\label{sec:discussion}
4	
5	\paragraph{Why per-DOF conformal features fail.}
6	The negative result on conformal conditioning (Section~\ref{sec:ablation}) warrants explanation.
7	% exp_id: perdof_eps0.1_object → 11.0%, obs_only_eps0.1_object → 19.0%
8	At $K{=}10$ samples in $d{=}6$ dimensions, the dimension-to-sample ratio $d/K = 0.6$ means that even with Ledoit--Wolf shrinkage~\cite{ledoit2004well}, per-DOF statistics (standard deviations, conformal radii) carry substantial estimation noise.
9	The frozen observation embedding $\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{obs}} \in \mathbb{R}^{960}$ already encodes the geometric information these features attempt to capture: object identity, spatial layout, and gripper-object relationship.
10	Adding noisy low-dimensional statistics to a rich representation does not improve the CRM's predictions; it degrades them.
11	
12	A subtler issue is the gap between offline and closed-loop metrics.
13	All CRM variants achieve low per-step MSE on held-out demonstration data, yet offline prediction accuracy does not predict closed-loop success.
14	In closed-loop execution, compounding error~\cite{ross2011dagger} shifts the state distribution away from training data, and the noisy conformal features amplify this drift: occasional erroneous correction directions, even when small in magnitude, can trigger cascading failures in individual episodes.
15	This challenges the intuition from controlled synthetic settings that richer uncertainty features improve corrections, and raises an open question: would larger $K$ (e.g., $K{=}50$) yield reliable enough covariance estimates for per-DOF features to help?
16	We cannot test this due to the $K$-fold increase in VLA forward passes per control step.
17	
18	\paragraph{When does action-level correction help?}
19	% exp_id: obs_only_eps0.1_object → 19.0%, argmin_K10_object → 12.0%
20	% exp_id: obs_only_eps0.1_spatial → 35.0%, argmin_K10_spatial → 34.0%

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_8/projects/acr-vla-conformal-refinement/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
  "content": "\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why per-DOF conformal features fail.}\nAt $K{=}10$ samples in $d{=}6$ dimensions, per-DOF statistics carry substantial estimation noise despite Ledoit--Wolf shrinkage~\\cite{ledoit2004well}.\nThe frozen observation embedding $\\mathbf{z}_{\\mathrm{obs}} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{960}$ already encodes the geometric information these features attempt to capture.\nAdding noisy low-dimensional statistics to this rich representation degrades CRM predictions rather than improving them.\nA subtler issue is the gap between offline and closed-loop metrics: all CRM variants achieve low per-step MSE on held-out data, yet offline accuracy does not predict closed-loop success.\nIn closed-loop execution, compounding error~\\cite{ross2011dagger} shifts the state distribution, and noisy conformal features amplify this drift.\n\n\\paragraph{When does action-level correction help?}\nThe +6.3\\,pp gain on LIBERO-Object versus the null result on LIBERO-Spatial reveals a structural distinction.\nObject perturbations create systematic action biases that a learned module can compensate from observation features.\nSpatial perturbations require higher-level re-planning; action-level residuals cannot redirect toward a different goal location.\nThe benefit of post-hoc correction depends on whether the distribution shift manifests as correctable motor error or as a planning-level failure.\n\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep]\n  \\item \\emph{Simulation only.} All experiments use LIBERO~\\cite{liu2023libero}; real-robot transfer is untested.\n  \\item \\emph{Single VLA backbone.} We evaluate only SmolVLA~\\cite{shukor2025smolvla} (450M parameters). Whether the pipeline transfers to larger VLAs (e.g., $\\pi_0$) is unknown.\n  \\item \\emph{Limited benchmarks.} Two LIBERO suites (20 tasks total); other benchmarks and perturbation types are not evaluated.\n  \\item \\emph{Per-task variance.} On individual tasks, obs\\_only occasionally underperforms the Argmin baseline.\n  \\item \\emph{Magnitude bound requires tuning.} We use $\\varepsilon{=}0.2$ throughout, but the optimal $\\varepsilon$ likely varies across architectures and task domains.\n  \\item \\emph{Seed variance.} Across six seeds, obs\\_only ranges from 15\\% to 22\\% (mean $17.5 \\pm 2.8$\\%).\n  \\item \\emph{Absolute performance.} 19.0\\% success is far from deployment-ready.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe presented a sample-then-correct pipeline that wraps a frozen flow-matching VLA with $L_2$ argmin selection and a lightweight residual correction module (${\\sim}$327K parameters, ${\\sim}$0.3\\,ms overhead).\nOn LIBERO-Object, the pipeline significantly improves over VLA-only (6-seed mean $17.5 \\pm 2.8$\\% vs.\\ $11.2 \\pm 1.6$\\%, $p = 0.0074$), with correction contributing ${\\sim}$5\\,pp and selection ${\\sim}$1\\,pp.\nOn LIBERO-Spatial, correction provides no benefit over selection, bounding the scope to object-centric manipulation.\nOur central negative result is that per-DOF conformal features do not improve correction over observation-only conditioning at the $K{=}10$ sample budget, a cautionary finding for uncertainty-conditioned action correction.\n"
}
```

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:intro}
3	
4	% --- Para 1: VLAs are impressive but fragile under distribution shift ---
5	Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) that generate actions via flow matching~\cite{black2024pi0,shukor2025smolvla,lipman2023flow} have reached near-saturation on in-distribution benchmarks such as LIBERO~\cite{liu2023libero}.
6	Yet this performance masks severe fragility: visual and physical perturbations common in deployment (camera viewpoint shifts, object layout changes, robot initial-state variation) routinely degrade success by over 65 percentage points.
7	On the LIBERO+ benchmark~\cite{fei2025liberoplus}, $\pi_0$ drops from 94.2\% to 6.6\% under initial-state perturbation, and UniVLA drops from 95.2\% to 4.3\% under viewpoint changes.
8	Closing this robustness gap without retraining the base policy is a prerequisite for deploying VLAs outside tightly controlled laboratory settings.
9	
10	% --- Para 2: Existing approaches and the gap ---
11	Two lines of work address this challenge.
12	Training-time methods improve the base policy through robustness-aware objectives~\cite{srpo2025,robustvla2025,digflow2025} or test-time reinforcement learning~\cite{liu2026ttvla}, but require retraining or per-task RL and remain bounded by the training distribution.
13	Inference-time methods leave the policy frozen: ReconVLA~\cite{chen2026reconvla} applies conformal quantile regression for uncertainty quantification and out-of-distribution detection, but does not learn corrective residuals; A2C2~\cite{a2c2_2025} trains a per-step correction head on frozen VLA action chunks, but applies correction unconditionally without multi-sample selection or explicit magnitude control; VLA-Pilot~\cite{li2025vlapilot} applies evolutionary optimization over action samples, but incurs 2.41\,s per control step.
14	Despite growing interest in inference-time action refinement, fundamental design questions remain unanswered: how much does candidate selection contribute relat
…[truncated 5292 chars]

1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related_work}
2	
3	\subsection{VLA Robustness}
4	
5	Methods for improving VLA robustness divide into training-time and inference-time approaches.
6	
7	\paragraph{Training-time methods.}
8	SRPO~\cite{srpo2025} applies self-referential RL fine-tuning and uses the policy's own successful rollouts as reward references for failed trajectories.
9	RobustVLA~\cite{robustvla2025} combines RL post-training with Jacobian and smoothness regularization to stabilize predictions under observation noise.
10	DiG-Flow~\cite{digflow2025} uses distributional discrepancy between observation and action embeddings as a gating signal to rebalance representation-level control.
11	TT-VLA~\cite{liu2026ttvla} adapts VLA parameters at test time through RL with progress rewards, but requires per-task reward design and gradient updates during deployment.
12	All four methods require retraining, reward engineering, or per-task RL, and cannot serve as drop-in improvements to an arbitrary frozen VLA.
13	
14	\paragraph{Inference-time methods.}
15	ReconVLA~\cite{chen2026reconvla} applies conformal quantile regression to calibrate per-action uncertainty bounds and Mahalanobis distance for out-of-distribution detection, but does not learn corrective residuals.
16	SC-VLA~\cite{liu2026scvla} performs online action refinement through sparse world imagination combined with residual RL, which still requires task-level RL training for the correction policy.
17	VLA-Pilot~\cite{li2025vlapilot} applies evolutionary diffusion optimization guided by an MLLM verifier at the cost of 2.41\,s per control step, prohibitive for real-time manipulation.
18	A2C2~\cite{a2c2_2025} learns a lightweight correction head that corrects each action within a pre-generated chunk at every control step to restore closed-loop responsiveness. It applies correction unconditionally, without first selecting a high-quality starting action or controlling correction magnitude.
19

### Tool result

_source line 2060 · 2026-05-18T10:56:04.502600+00:00_

1	Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models for robotic manipulation degrade under distribution shift, yet improving their performance without retraining remains underexplored.
2	We propose a sample-select-correct pipeline for inference-time action refinement of frozen flow-matching VLAs that draws multiple action samples, selects the most representative candidate via $L_2$ argmin, and applies a learned residual correction bounded in magnitude.
3	The correction module is a ${\sim}$327K-parameter MLP conditioned on the frozen VLA's observation embedding, trained offline with supervised $L_2$ loss on demonstration data.
4	On the LIBERO-Object benchmark (10 tasks, 6 seeds), the pipeline achieves $17.5 \pm 2.8$\% versus $11.2 \pm 1.6$\% for the unmodified VLA (+6.3\,pp, $p = 0.0074$).
5	Component decomposition reveals that learned correction accounts for ${\sim}$5\,pp and sample selection for ${\sim}$1\,pp; neither component alone improves the baseline.
6	A systematic conditioning and magnitude study yields two findings: observation features produce robust corrections across all tested magnitude bounds (13.5--17.5\%), while per-DOF conformal uncertainty features cause progressive collapse (14\% to 0\%) as the bound increases.
7	We further show that multi-sample correction requires stochastic action diversity: tokenized VLAs produce near-identical samples, establishing an architectural applicability boundary.
8

  "old_string": "% --- Para 4: Three key findings ---\nWe evaluate the pipeline on LIBERO-Object and LIBERO-Spatial (10 tasks, 100 trials each) using SmolVLA~\\cite{shukor2025smolvla} as the frozen backbone.\nThree findings emerge.\n\n\\textbf{(1) The full pipeline significantly improves over the frozen VLA, with correction as the dominant factor.}\nWith observation-only conditioning at $\\varepsilon{=}0.2$, the pipeline achieves 17.5\\% mean success across 6 seeds versus 11.2\\% for VLA-only across 5 seeds (+6.3\\,pp, pooled Fisher's exact $p = 0.0074$, Cohen's $d = 1.21$).\nComponent decomposition reveals that correction accounts for ${\\sim}$5\\,pp and selection for ${\\sim}$1\\,pp: at $K{=}1$ (correction without selection), success is 10.3\\%, at the VLA-only level; argmin selection alone yields 12.4\\%.\nNeither component suffices in isolation.\nOn LIBERO-Spatial, all methods cluster within 30--35\\%, a null result that bounds the scope of action-level correction to object-centric manipulation (Section~\\ref{sec:main_results}, Table~\\ref{tab:main}).\n\n\\textbf{(2) Observation features suffice for correction; conformal uncertainty features hurt.}\nWe compare three CRM conditioning variants at fixed $\\varepsilon{=}0.1$: observation-only conditioning (19\\%, seed 42) substantially outperforms per-DOF conformal conditioning (11\\%), argmin selection alone (12\\%), and scalar uncertainty conditioning (9\\%).\nThe observation-only variant is also robust across magnitude bounds, maintaining 13.5--17.5\\% for all tested $\\varepsilon$, while per-DOF conditioning collapses from 14\\% to 0\\% as $\\varepsilon$ increases.\nThe root cause is noisy covariance estimation from only $K{=}10$ samples in 6 dimensions (Section~\\ref{sec:ablation}, Section~\\ref{sec:magnitude_results}).\n\n\\textbf{(3) Multi-sample correction requires stochastic action diversity.}\nSmolVLA (flow-matching) produces diverse samples suitable for selection and correction.\nOpenVLA~\\cite{kim2024openvla} (autoregressive tokenization) generates 8 out of 10 identical samples with per-DOF variance below $10^{-30}$.\nThis establishes an architectural applicability boundary: the pipeline is specific to stochastic generative VLAs (Section~\\ref{sec:applicability}, Figure~\\ref{fig:diversity}).",
  "new_string": "% --- Para 4: Key findings ---\nWe evaluate the pipeline on LIBERO-Object and LIBERO-Spatial (10 tasks each) using SmolVLA~\\cite{shukor2025smolvla} as the frozen backbone.\nOn LIBERO-Object, the pipeline achieves $17.5 \\pm 2.8$\\% (6 seeds) versus $11.2 \\pm 1.6$\\% for VLA-only ($p = 0.0074$), with correction contributing ${\\sim}$5\\,pp and selection ${\\sim}$1\\,pp; neither suffices alone.\nOn LIBERO-Spatial, all methods cluster within 30--35\\%, bounding the scope to object-centric manipulation.\nA conditioning study reveals that observation features produce robust corrections across all magnitude bounds, while per-DOF conformal features cause progressive collapse (14\\%$\\to$0\\%).\nWe further show that the pipeline requires stochastic action diversity: tokenized VLAs~\\cite{kim2024openvla} produce near-identical samples, establishing an architectural applicability boundary."
}

  "old_string": "Our contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]\n  \\item \\textbf{A multi-sample inference-time correction pipeline with component decomposition.}\n  We propose a sample-select-correct pipeline that combines $L_2$ argmin selection with bounded residual correction for frozen flow-matching VLAs, and decompose the pipeline into selection (${\\sim}$1\\,pp) and correction (${\\sim}$5\\,pp) contributions.\n  The full pipeline achieves a statistically significant +6.3\\,pp improvement over the frozen VLA baseline ($p = 0.0074$, 6 seeds) on LIBERO-Object (Section~\\ref{sec:main_results}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{First systematic conditioning and magnitude study, with an honest negative on conformal features.}\n  We compare three conditioning variants across a $6{\\times}$ range of magnitude bounds and find that observation-only conditioning is robust across all settings, while per-DOF conformal features and scalar uncertainty are harmful.\n  This challenges the intuition that richer uncertainty features improve correction (Section~\\ref{sec:ablation}, Section~\\ref{sec:magnitude_results}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Applicability boundary: stochastic diversity as a prerequisite.}\n  We show that tokenized VLAs lack the sample diversity required for multi-sample correction and that action-level correction does not transfer to spatial reasoning tasks, establishing clear scope boundaries for inference-time action refinement (Section~\\ref{sec:applicability}).\n\\end{enumerate}",
  "new_string": "Our contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]\n  \\item A sample-select-correct pipeline for frozen flow-matching VLAs with component decomposition: correction (${\\sim}$5\\,pp) dominates selection (${\\sim}$1\\,pp), and the full pipeline yields +6.3\\,pp ($p = 0.0074$, 6 seeds) on LIBERO-Object.\n  \\item A systematic conditioning and magnitude study showing that observation-only conditioning is robust across all tested bounds, while conformal uncertainty features are harmful.\n  \\item Applicability boundaries: multi-sample correction requires stochastic action diversity (absent in tokenized VLAs) and does not transfer to spatial reasoning tasks.\n\\end{enumerate}"
}

    "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_8/projects/acr-vla-conformal-refinement/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "old_string": "Our contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]\n  \\item \\textbf{A multi-sample inference-time correction pipeline with component decomposition.}\n  We propose a sample-select-correct pipeline that combines $L_2$ argmin selection with bounded residual correction for frozen flow-matching VLAs, and decompose the pipeline into selection (${\\sim}$1\\,pp) and correction (${\\sim}$5\\,pp) contributions.\n  The full pipeline achieves a statistically significant +6.3\\,pp improvement over the frozen VLA baseline ($p = 0.0074$, 6 seeds) on LIBERO-Object (Section~\\ref{sec:main_results}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{First systematic conditioning and magnitude study, with an honest negative on conformal features.}\n  We compare three conditioning variants across a $6{\\times}$ range of magnitude bounds and find that observation-only conditioning is robust across all settings, while per-DOF conformal features and scalar uncertainty are harmful.\n  This challenges the intuition that richer uncertainty features improve correction (Section~\\ref{sec:ablation}, Section~\\ref{sec:magnitude_results}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Applicability boundary: stochastic diversity as a prerequisite.}\n  We show that tokenized VLAs lack the sample diversity required for multi-sample correction and that action-level correction does not transfer to spatial reasoning tasks, establishing clear scope boundaries for inference-time action refinement (Section~\\ref{sec:applicability}).\n\\end{enumerate}",
    "new_string": "Our contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]\n  \\item A sample-select-correct pipeline for frozen flow-matching VLAs with component decomposition: correction (${\\sim}$5\\,pp) dominates selection (${\\sim}$1\\,pp), and the full pipeline yields +6.3\\,pp ($p = 0.0074$, 6 seeds) on LIBERO-Object.\n  \\item A systematic conditioning and magnitude study showing that observation-only conditioning is robust across all tested bounds, while conformal uncertainty features are harmful.\n  \\item Applicability boundaries: multi-sample correction requires stochastic action diversity (absent in tokenized VLAs) and does not transfer to spatial reasoning tasks.\n\\end{enumerate}"
  }
}
```

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_8/projects/acr-vla-conformal-refinement/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "A2C2 is the closest concurrent work to ours: both attach a lightweight correction module to a frozen VLA without retraining the base policy. The key differences are threefold.\nFirst, our pipeline operates as a two-stage process, where multi-sample selection (choosing the candidate closest to the sample mean by $L_2$ distance) precedes bounded residual correction, whereas A2C2 applies per-step correction directly without a selection stage.\nSecond, our pipeline enforces explicit magnitude control via $L_2$ norm clipping, which our ablations show is critical for preventing compounding correction errors (Section~\\ref{sec:magnitude_results}).\nThird, our pipeline conditions the correction module on the frozen VLA's observation embedding rather than temporal action features.\nOur perdof ablation variant additionally conditions on conformal uncertainty features~\\cite{braun2025cgs,angelopoulos2023gentle}, but observation-only conditioning proves sufficient and more robust across magnitude bounds (Section~\\ref{sec:ablation}).",
  "new_string": "A2C2 is the closest concurrent work: both attach a lightweight correction module to a frozen VLA.\nOur pipeline differs in three ways: (1) multi-sample selection precedes correction, (2) explicit $L_2$ norm clipping controls correction magnitude (Section~\\ref{sec:magnitude_results}), and (3) the correction module conditions on the frozen VLA's observation embedding rather than temporal action features.\nWe additionally ablate conformal uncertainty conditioning~\\cite{braun2025cgs,angelopoulos2023gentle} and find observation-only features sufficient (Section~\\ref{sec:ablation})."
}
```

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_8/projects/acr-vla-conformal-refinement/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "\\subsection{Inference-Time Compute Scaling}\n\nTest-time compute scaling has emerged as a powerful axis for improving language model performance without additional training.\nSnell et al.~\\cite{snell2024scaling} show that allocating more compute at inference time through best-of-N sampling with a verifier can match or exceed the gains from larger models and establish a compute-optimal framework for test-time scaling.\nRoVer~\\cite{dai2025rover} extends this to reward-free settings by using self-verification to select among multiple candidate responses without an explicit reward model.\nDA-SIP~\\cite{chun2025dasip} introduces decision-aware sample importance weighting that connects optimal sample selection to downstream task loss rather than generic quality metrics.\n\nThis paradigm is beginning to transfer to embodied AI, where drawing multiple action samples from a stochastic policy is analogous to repeated sampling from a language model.\nOur pipeline instantiates this connection: at each control step, it draws $K{=}10$ samples from the frozen VLA and selects the candidate closest to the sample mean.\nThe K-value curve shows monotonic improvement from $K{=}1$ to $K{=}5$ to $K{=}10$ (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}), consistent with the scaling behavior observed in language model test-time compute.\nThe key difference from language model settings is the absence of a learned verifier.\nOur pipeline replaces the verifier with a statistical selection criterion ($L_2$ argmin) followed by a learned correction step, a design point that may prove relevant as multi-sample VLA inference becomes more common.",
  "new_string": "\\subsection{Inference-Time Compute Scaling}\n\nTest-time compute scaling improves language model outputs through best-of-N sampling with verifiers~\\cite{snell2024scaling}, self-verification~\\cite{dai2025rover}, or decision-aware sample weighting~\\cite{chun2025dasip}.\nOur pipeline transfers this paradigm to embodied AI: drawing $K{=}10$ action samples and selecting the best candidate is analogous to repeated LLM sampling, with $L_2$ argmin replacing a learned verifier.\nPerformance increases monotonically with $K$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:k_scaling}), consistent with LLM test-time scaling."
}
```

</details>

    "old_string": "\\subsection{Inference-Time Compute Scaling}\n\nTest-time compute scaling has emerged as a powerful axis for improving language model performance without additional training.\nSnell et al.~\\cite{snell2024scaling} show that allocating more compute at inference time through best-of-N sampling with a verifier can match or exceed the gains from larger models and establish a compute-optimal framework for test-time scaling.\nRoVer~\\cite{dai2025rover} extends this to reward-free settings by using self-verification to select among multiple candidate responses without an explicit reward model.\nDA-SIP~\\cite{chun2025dasip} introduces decision-aware sample importance weighting that connects optimal sample selection to downstream task loss rather than generic quality metrics.\n\nThis paradigm is beginning to transfer to embodied AI, where drawing multiple action samples from a stochastic policy is analogous to repeated sampling from a language model.\nOur pipeline instantiates this connection: at each control step, it draws $K{=}10$ samples from the frozen VLA and selects the candidate closest to the sample mean.\nThe K-value curve shows monotonic improvement from $K{=}1$ to $K{=}5$ to $K{=}10$ (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}), consistent with the scaling behavior observed in language model test-time compute.\nThe key difference from language model settings is the absence of a learned verifier.\nOur pipeline replaces the verifier with a statistical selection criterion ($L_2$ argmin) followed by a learned correction step, a design point that may prove relevant as multi-sample VLA inference becomes more common.",

_source line 2069 · 2026-05-18T10:57:38.877388+00:00_

called Edit

<details open><summary>🔧 Tool call — Edit</summary>

```json
{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_8/projects/acr-vla-conformal-refinement/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "Residual policy learning augments a base controller with a learned additive correction.\nSilver et al.~\\cite{silver2018residual} introduced the framework for classical controllers with zero-initialization to ensure the learned residual starts from the base policy's behavior.\nSubsequent work has focused on controlling residual magnitude, since unconstrained corrections lead to compounding error and policy collapse.\nTRANSIC~\\cite{jiang2024transic} uses a learned binary gate conditioned on the current state to selectively enable sim-to-real residual actions; ungated correction causes model collapse.\nPLD~\\cite{xiao2026pld} freezes the VLA backbone and trains lightweight residual actors with bounded corrections ($[-\\xi, \\xi]$) via off-policy RL, then distills improved trajectories back into the base model.\nDRC~\\cite{pan2025drc} applies exponential decay to each expert correction event so that corrective signals persist and fade over time.\n\nMagnitude control is the key connection to our method.\nSilver et al.\\ use zero-initialization, TRANSIC uses binary gating, PLD uses fixed action bounds, and DRC uses exponential decay.\nOur pipeline uses fixed $L_2$ norm clipping, the simplest possible scheme, yet our ablations show that the choice of clipping threshold $\\varepsilon$ has an outsized effect on performance, with the optimal value varying across task suites (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\nThis suggests that magnitude control in VLA inference-time correction is an underexplored design axis.\nA second distinction separates our method from all four: each requires RL training (reward functions, environment interaction, or on-policy rollouts), whereas our correction module is trained with supervised $L_2$ loss on offline demonstration data, with no need for reward design or environment access.",
  "new_string": "Residual policy learning augments a base controller with a learned correction~\\cite{silver2018residual}.\nMagnitude control is the central challenge: TRANSIC~\\cite{jiang2024transic} gates residuals with a learned binary switch, PLD~\\cite{xiao2026pld} bounds corrections within $[-\\xi, \\xi]$ via off-policy RL, and DRC~\\cite{pan2025drc} applies exponential decay to correction signals.\nOur pipeline uses fixed $L_2$ norm clipping and trains with supervised loss on demonstration data, requiring no RL, reward design, or environment interaction."
}
```

</details>

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_8/projects/acr-vla-conformal-refinement/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Why does richer conditioning fail?}\nWe identify three factors:\n\n\\emph{(1) Noisy covariance estimation.}\nWith $K{=}10$ samples in $d{=}6$ dimensions, the dimension-to-sample ratio is $d/K = 0.6$.\nWhile Ledoit--Wolf shrinkage~\\cite{ledoit2004well} stabilizes the covariance estimate for selection (Section~\\ref{sec:selection}), the per-DOF features derived from this estimate (standard deviations, conformal radii) still carry substantial estimation noise.\nFeeding these noisy features as CRM input introduces a distribution shift between training data (where noise patterns are consistent) and closed-loop evaluation (where compounding state-distribution drift produces different noise patterns).\n\n\\emph{(2) Observation features are sufficient.}\nThe frozen VLA observation embedding $\\mathbf{z}_{\\mathrm{obs}} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{960}$ already encodes task-relevant geometric information: object identity, spatial layout, gripper-object relationship, and task progress.\nThis information subsumes what the per-DOF conformal features attempt to capture, namely which action dimensions are uncertain given the current scene.\nThe CRM learns to map directly from scene understanding to corrective residuals, without the intermediate conformal statistics.\n\n\\emph{(3) Per-step MSE $\\neq$ episode success.}\nAll CRM variants achieve low per-step MSE on held-out demonstration data, yet offline prediction accuracy does not predict closed-loop task success.\nIn closed-loop evaluation, small per-step errors compound and the robot visits states far from the demonstration distribution~\\cite{ross2011dagger}.\nThe noisy conformal features amplify this drift: occasional erroneous correction directions, even when small in magnitude, can trigger cascading failures in individual episodes.\n\n\\paragraph{Connection to magnitude sensitivity.}\nThe obs\\_only variant is also more robust across magnitude bounds $\\varepsilon$ than perdof (Section~\\ref{sec:magnitude_results}), providing further evidence that observation-only conditioning yields a more stable correction signal.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Why does richer conditioning fail?}\nThree factors contribute.\nFirst, with $K{=}10$ samples in $d{=}6$ dimensions, per-DOF statistics (standard deviations, conformal radii) carry substantial estimation noise despite Ledoit--Wolf shrinkage~\\cite{ledoit2004well}; this noise compounds differently in closed-loop evaluation than in offline training.\nSecond, the frozen observation embedding $\\mathbf{z}_{\\mathrm{obs}} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{960}$ already encodes the geometric information that conformal features attempt to capture.\nThird, all CRM variants achieve similar per-step MSE on held-out data, but noisy conformal features amplify compounding errors~\\cite{ross2011dagger} in closed-loop execution.\nThe obs\\_only variant is also more robust across magnitude bounds (Section~\\ref{sec:magnitude_results})."
}
```

<details open><summary>🔧 Tool call — Edit</summary>

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_8/projects/acr-vla-conformal-refinement/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{obs\\_only is robust across all magnitudes.}\nThe obs\\_only variant maintains 13.5--17.5\\% across $\\varepsilon \\in [0.03, \\text{uncapped}]$, peaking at 17.5\\% ($\\varepsilon{=}0.2$, 6 seeds) and remaining above VLA-only (11.2\\%) at every tested bound, including uncapped (14.3\\%, 3 seeds).\nThe practitioner need not carefully tune $\\varepsilon$: any value in $[0.05, 0.3]$ yields comparable performance.\n\n\\paragraph{perdof collapses progressively.}\nThe perdof variant starts at 14.0\\% ($\\varepsilon{=}0.03$, $n{=}1$), drops to 11.0\\% at $\\varepsilon{=}0.1$ (6 seeds), and collapses to 0\\% at the uncapped setting (3 seeds, all zero).\nThis progressive degradation traces to noisy covariance estimation: with only $K{=}10$ samples in $d{=}6$ dimensions, per-DOF features carry substantial estimation noise despite Ledoit--Wolf shrinkage.\nAt tight $\\varepsilon$, clipping masks the noisy correction directions; as the bound loosens, erroneous corrections compound across the trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Scalar conditioning is worse than no conditioning.}\nThe scalar variant achieves 8.5\\% (4 seeds at $\\varepsilon{=}0.1$), below VLA-only (11.2\\%).\nThis confirms that conformal statistics at $K{=}10$ are unreliable regardless of aggregation dimensionality: the noise originates in the poorly conditioned covariance estimate, not in how the scores are decomposed.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical recommendation.}\nWe use $\\varepsilon{=}0.2$ as the primary setting.\nWithin $[0.05, 0.3]$, obs\\_only never degrades below the VLA-only baseline across all tested seeds.",
  "new_string": "obs\\_only maintains 13.5--17.5\\% across $\\varepsilon \\in [0.03, \\text{uncapped}]$, peaking at 17.5\\% ($\\varepsilon{=}0.2$, 6 seeds) and remaining above VLA-only (11.2\\%) at every tested bound.\nIn contrast, perdof collapses from 14.0\\% ($\\varepsilon{=}0.03$) to 0\\% (uncapped, 3 seeds), because loosening the bound exposes noisy correction directions from poorly estimated covariances.\nThe scalar variant (8.5\\%, 4 seeds at $\\varepsilon{=}0.1$) falls below VLA-only, confirming that conformal statistics at $K{=}10$ are unreliable regardless of aggregation.\nWe use $\\varepsilon{=}0.2$ as the primary setting; any value in $[0.05, 0.3]$ yields comparable obs\\_only performance."
}
```

    "old_string": "\\paragraph{obs\\_only is robust across all magnitudes.}\nThe obs\\_only variant maintains 13.5--17.5\\% across $\\varepsilon \\in [0.03, \\text{uncapped}]$, peaking at 17.5\\% ($\\varepsilon{=}0.2$, 6 seeds) and remaining above VLA-only (11.2\\%) at every tested bound, including uncapped (14.3\\%, 3 seeds).\nThe practitioner need not carefully tune $\\varepsilon$: any value in $[0.05, 0.3]$ yields comparable performance.\n\n\\paragraph{perdof collapses progressively.}\nThe perdof variant starts at 14.0\\% ($\\varepsilon{=}0.03$, $n{=}1$), drops to 11.0\\% at $\\varepsilon{=}0.1$ (6 seeds), and collapses to 0\\% at the uncapped setting (3 seeds, all zero).\nThis progressive degradation traces to noisy covariance estimation: with only $K{=}10$ samples in $d{=}6$ dimensions, per-DOF features carry substantial estimation noise despite Ledoit--Wolf shrinkage.\nAt tight $\\varepsilon$, clipping masks the noisy correction directions; as the bound loosens, erroneous corrections compound across the trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Scalar conditioning is worse than no conditioning.}\nThe scalar variant achieves 8.5\\% (4 seeds at $\\varepsilon{=}0.1$), below VLA-only (11.2\\%).\nThis confirms that conformal statistics at $K{=}10$ are unreliable regardless of aggregation dimensionality: the noise originates in the poorly conditioned covariance estimate, not in how the scores are decomposed.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical recommendation.}\nWe use $\\varepsilon{=}0.2$ as the primary setting.\nWithin $[0.05, 0.3]$, obs\\_only never degrades below the VLA-only baseline across all tested seeds.",
    "new_string": "obs\\_only maintains 13.5--17.5\\% across $\\varepsilon \\in [0.03, \\text{uncapped}]$, peaking at 17.5\\% ($\\varepsilon{=}0.2$, 6 seeds) and remaining above VLA-only (11.2\\%) at every tested bound.\nIn contrast, perdof collapses from 14.0\\% ($\\varepsilon{=}0.03$) to 0\\% (uncapped, 3 seeds), because loosening the bound exposes noisy correction directions from poorly estimated covariances.\nThe scalar variant (8.5\\%, 4 seeds at $\\varepsilon{=}0.1$) falls below VLA-only, confirming that conformal statistics at $K{=}10$ are unreliable regardless of aggregation.\nWe use $\\varepsilon{=}0.2$ as the primary setting; any value in $[0.05, 0.3]$ yields comparable obs\\_only performance."
  }
}
```

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_8/projects/acr-vla-conformal-refinement/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Pipeline decomposition: selection and correction are synergistic.}\nTo isolate contributions, we evaluate the CRM without multi-sample selection ($K{=}1$): the CRM corrects a single VLA output directly.\nAt $K{=}1$, CRM correction achieves 10.3\\% (3-seed mean), at the VLA-only level (11.2\\%).\nArgmin selection without correction achieves 12.4\\% (5-seed mean), a modest +1.2\\,pp gain.\nNeither component alone meaningfully improves the frozen VLA, yet the full pipeline ($K{=}10$, selection + correction) achieves 17.5\\%, a +6.3\\,pp gain ($p = 0.0074$).\nThis synergy arises because argmin selection filters out the worst samples, providing the CRM with a higher-quality starting point; the CRM then applies corrections that are more reliable on these pre-screened candidates.\nIn terms of contribution decomposition: selection accounts for ${\\sim}$1\\,pp and correction for ${\\sim}$5\\,pp, with correction 4--5$\\times$ more valuable than selection alone.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Pipeline decomposition.}\nAt $K{=}1$ (correction without selection), CRM achieves 10.3\\% (3-seed mean), at the VLA-only level.\nArgmin selection alone yields 12.4\\% (5-seed mean).\nNeither component alone improves the baseline, yet the full pipeline ($K{=}10$) achieves 17.5\\% ($p = 0.0074$): selection provides a higher-quality starting point, and the CRM applies corrections that are more reliable on pre-screened candidates.\nCorrection accounts for ${\\sim}$5\\,pp and selection for ${\\sim}$1\\,pp."
}
```

</details>

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Spatial benchmark: correction does not help.}\nOn LIBERO-Spatial, all methods cluster within a 5\\,pp band (30--35\\%).\nThe best CRM variants (obs\\_only, perdof) achieve 35\\%, only 1\\,pp above the Argmin baseline (34\\%).\nThis is a null result that we report prominently.\n\nWe interpret this as follows: spatial perturbations in LIBERO-Spatial involve changes to object layout (e.g., moving target locations), which may require re-planning rather than local action correction.\nThe VLA's observation encoder cannot extract correctable error signals from spatial shifts. The problem is not that actions are inaccurate given the current state, but that the task structure itself has changed.\nResidual correction at the action level is the wrong granularity for this type of generalization.\n\n\\paragraph{Key finding.}\nThe full pipeline significantly improves over VLA-only for object manipulation ($p < 0.01$), where the challenge is fine-grained motor control with novel objects.\nFor spatial reasoning, where the challenge is planning under layout changes, correction at the action level provides no benefit over selection alone.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Spatial benchmark: correction does not help.}\nOn LIBERO-Spatial, all methods cluster within 30--35\\%.\nSpatial perturbations involve layout changes that require re-planning rather than local action correction; residual correction at the action level is the wrong granularity.\nThe pipeline improves over VLA-only for object manipulation ($p < 0.01$) but not for spatial reasoning."
}

{

  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_8/projects/acr-vla-conformal-refinement/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "This ordering is the central negative result of our ablation: per-DOF conformal features, despite being intuitively richer (40 additional dimensions capturing per-DOF standard deviations, conformal radii, and log-volume terms), \\emph{hurt} rather than help.\nScalar uncertainty conditioning (1 additional dimension) performs even worse, falling 3.0\\,pp below the Argmin baseline that uses no correction at all.\n\nThe scalar $<$ perdof ordering is consistent across seeds (scalar: 9\\%/6\\% for seeds 42/44; perdof: 11\\%/8\\%).\nOne-dimensional scalar uncertainty compresses all six action dimensions into a single number.\nWhen certain dimensions carry high uncertainty but the correction direction is already correct along those axes, the scalar signal cannot distinguish this case from one where correction is needed everywhere.\nThe result is uniform over-correction or under-correction across all dimensions.\nPer-DOF conditioning at least permits dimension-specific judgments, though at $K{=}10$ these judgments remain too noisy to be useful.\nEven 1D scalar uncertainty conditioning is harmful, confirming that conformal scores at $K{=}10$ are unreliable regardless of aggregation dimensionality.\nThe noise originates in the covariance estimate itself (poorly conditioned with only 10 samples in 6D), not in how the scores are decomposed across dimensions.",
  "new_string": "Per-DOF conformal features (40 additional dimensions) \\emph{hurt} rather than help, and scalar uncertainty (1 dimension) performs even worse, falling 3.0\\,pp below the no-correction baseline.\nThe ordering is consistent across seeds (scalar: 9\\%/6\\% for seeds 42/44; perdof: 11\\%/8\\%), confirming that conformal scores at $K{=}10$ are unreliable regardless of aggregation dimensionality."
}
```

The selection operates independently of the downstream correction module: it is a statistical denoising step that provides the CRM with a higher-quality starting point.",

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_8/projects/acr-vla-conformal-refinement/docs/paper/method.tex",
  "old_string": "The CRM is trained offline on demonstration trajectories from LIBERO~\\cite{liu2023libero} with $L_2$ loss:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L} = \\bigl\\|\\mathbf{a}^* - \\mathbf{a}_{\\mathrm{expert}}\\bigr\\|_2^2,\n  \\label{eq:loss}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{a}_{\\mathrm{expert}}$ is the demonstration action. The training set comprises 26{,}737 precomputed samples from 10 demonstrations per task across 20 LIBERO tasks. For each demonstration step, we run $K{=}10$ forward passes through the frozen SmolVLA, apply argmin selection~(\\ref{eq:argmin}) to obtain $a_{\\mathrm{base}}$, and train the CRM to predict the residual that moves $a_{\\mathrm{base}}$ toward $\\mathbf{a}_{\\mathrm{expert}}$. Data augmentation includes additive Gaussian noise on the mean-action vector ($\\sigma \\in [0.02, 0.1]$) and, for variants that receive uncertainty inputs, noise on those features ($\\sigma \\approx 0.006$). Both forms of augmentation improve generalization to the state distribution encountered during closed-loop evaluation, which diverges from the demonstration distribution due to compounding errors~\\cite{ross2011dagger}.",
  "new_string": "The CRM is trained offline on demonstration trajectories from LIBERO~\\cite{liu2023libero} with $L_2$ loss:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L} = \\bigl\\|\\mathbf{a}^* - \\mathbf{a}_{\\mathrm{expert}}\\bigr\\|_2^2,\n  \\label{eq:loss}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{a}_{\\mathrm{expert}}$ is the demonstration action. The training set comprises 26{,}737 precomputed samples from 10 demonstrations per task across 20 LIBERO tasks. For each demonstration step, we run $K{=}10$ forward passes through the frozen SmolVLA, apply argmin selection~(\\ref{eq:argmin}) to obtain $a_{\\mathrm{base}}$, and train the CRM to predict the residual toward $\\mathbf{a}_{\\mathrm{expert}}$. Data augmentation (Gaussian noise on $a_{\\mathrm{base}}$ and uncertainty features) improves generalization to the closed-loop state distribution~\\cite{ross2011dagger}."
}

    "old_string": "The CRM is trained offline on demonstration trajectories from LIBERO~\\cite{liu2023libero} with $L_2$ loss:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L} = \\bigl\\|\\mathbf{a}^* - \\mathbf{a}_{\\mathrm{expert}}\\bigr\\|_2^2,\n  \\label{eq:loss}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{a}_{\\mathrm{expert}}$ is the demonstration action. The training set comprises 26{,}737 precomputed samples from 10 demonstrations per task across 20 LIBERO tasks. For each demonstration step, we run $K{=}10$ forward passes through the frozen SmolVLA, apply argmin selection~(\\ref{eq:argmin}) to obtain $a_{\\mathrm{base}}$, and train the CRM to predict the residual that moves $a_{\\mathrm{base}}$ toward $\\mathbf{a}_{\\mathrm{expert}}$. Data augmentation includes additive Gaussian noise on the mean-action vector ($\\sigma \\in [0.02, 0.1]$) and, for variants that receive uncertainty inputs, noise on those features ($\\sigma \\approx 0.006$). Both forms of augmentation improve generalization to the state distribution encountered during closed-loop evaluation, which diverges from the demonstration distribution due to compounding errors~\\cite{ross2011dagger}.",
    "new_string": "The CRM is trained offline on demonstration trajectories from LIBERO~\\cite{liu2023libero} with $L_2$ loss:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L} = \\bigl\\|\\mathbf{a}^* - \\mathbf{a}_{\\mathrm{expert}}\\bigr\\|_2^2,\n  \\label{eq:loss}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{a}_{\\mathrm{expert}}$ is the demonstration action. The training set comprises 26{,}737 precomputed samples from 10 demonstrations per task across 20 LIBERO tasks. For each demonstration step, we run $K{=}10$ forward passes through the frozen SmolVLA, apply argmin selection~(\\ref{eq:argmin}) to obtain $a_{\\mathrm{base}}$, and train the CRM to predict the residual toward $\\mathbf{a}_{\\mathrm{expert}}$. Data augmentation (Gaussian noise on $a_{\\mathrm{base}}$ and uncertainty features) improves generalization to the closed-loop state distribution~\\cite{ross2011dagger}."
  }
}

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:intro}
3	
4	% --- Para 1: VLAs are impressive but fragile under distribution shift ---
5	Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) that generate actions via flow matching~\cite{black2024pi0,shukor2025smolvla,lipman2023flow} have reached near-saturation on in-distribution benchmarks such as LIBERO~\cite{liu2023libero}.
6	Yet this performance masks severe fragility: visual and physical perturbations common in deployment (camera viewpoint shifts, object layout changes, robot initial-state variation) routinely degrade success by over 65 percentage points.
7	On the LIBERO+ benchmark~\cite{fei2025liberoplus}, $\pi_0$ drops from 94.2\% to 6.6\% under initial-state perturbation, and UniVLA drops from 95.2\% to 4.3\% under viewpoint changes.
8	Closing this robustness gap without retraining the base policy is a prerequisite for deploying VLAs outside tightly controlled laboratory settings.
9	
10	% --- Para 2: Existing approaches and the gap ---
11	Two lines of work address this challenge.
12	Training-time methods improve the base policy through robustness-aware objectives~\cite{srpo2025,robustvla2025,digflow2025} or test-time reinforcement learning~\cite{liu2026ttvla}, but require retraining or per-task RL and remain bounded by the training distribution.
13	Inference-time methods leave the policy frozen: ReconVLA~\cite{chen2026reconvla} applies conformal quantile regression for uncertainty quantification and out-of-distribution detection, but does not learn corrective residuals; A2C2~\cite{a2c2_2025} trains a per-step correction head on frozen VLA action chunks, but applies correction unconditionally without multi-sample selection or explicit magnitude control; VLA-Pilot~\cite{li2025vlapilot} applies evolutionary optimization over action samples, but incurs 2.41\,s per control step.
14	Despite growing interest in inference-time action refinement, fundamental design questions remain unanswered: how much does candidate selection contribute relat

**CRITICAL: RoVer 误表述（related_work.tex line 45-46）**
正文写 RoVer 是 "reward-free" + "without an explicit reward model"，但引用论文标题明确写 "Robot **Reward Model** as Test-Time Verifier"。这是对引用工作的直接误表述，审稿人会立即识别。

修复：重写 RoVer 描述，准确反映其使用 reward model 作为 test-time verifier 的机制。如果你需要 reward-free 例子，换一篇论文或删除 "reward-free"。

**WARNING 1: related_work.tex line 37 "optimal value varying across task suites"**
我们只在 LIBERO-Object 上有有意义的 ε 效果，LIBERO-Spatial 是 null result。改为 "the choice of clipping threshold ε has an outsized effect on performance (Section X)"，删除跨 suite 变化的 claim。

**WARNING 2: line 50 K-value 单调性 claim**
K=5 只有 1 seed (14%)，"monotonic improvement" 统计不稳固。改为更谨慎措辞，如 "preliminary evidence of monotonic improvement" 或 "K=5 achieves 14% (1 seed), suggesting monotonic scaling"。K=5 seed43 正在跑，完成后更新。

**优先级**：CRITICAL 立即修，2 warnings 在 Step 5 迭代中处理。

1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related_work}
2	
3	\subsection{VLA Robustness}
4	
5	Methods for improving VLA robustness divide into training-time and inference-time approaches.
6	
7	\paragraph{Training-time methods.}
8	SRPO~\cite{srpo2025} applies self-referential RL fine-tuning and uses the policy's own successful rollouts as reward references for failed trajectories.
9	RobustVLA~\cite{robustvla2025} combines RL post-training with Jacobian and smoothness regularization to stabilize predictions under observation noise.
10	DiG-Flow~\cite{digflow2025} uses distributional discrepancy between observation and action embeddings as a gating signal to rebalance representation-level control.
11	TT-VLA~\cite{liu2026ttvla} adapts VLA parameters at test time through RL with progress rewards, but requires per-task reward design and gradient updates during deployment.
12	All four methods require retraining, reward engineering, or per-task RL, and cannot serve as drop-in improvements to an arbitrary frozen VLA.
13	
14	\paragraph{Inference-time methods.}
15	ReconVLA~\cite{chen2026reconvla} applies conformal quantile regression to calibrate per-action uncertainty bounds and Mahalanobis distance for out-of-distribution detection, but does not learn corrective residuals.
16	SC-VLA~\cite{liu2026scvla} performs online action refinement through sparse world imagination combined with residual RL, which still requires task-level RL training for the correction policy.
17	VLA-Pilot~\cite{li2025vlapilot} applies evolutionary diffusion optimization guided by an MLLM verifier at the cost of 2.41\,s per control step, prohibitive for real-time manipulation.
18	A2C2~\cite{a2c2_2025} learns a lightweight correction head that corrects each action within a pre-generated chunk at every control step to restore closed-loop responsiveness. It applies correction unconditionally, without first selecting a high-quality starting action or controlling correction magnitude.
19

9	\begin{figure}[h]
10	\centering
11	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/fig3_kvalue.pdf}
12	\caption{Success rate (\%) vs.\ number of VLA samples $K$ on LIBERO-Object (obs\_only, $\varepsilon{=}0.2$). Reference lines: VLA-only (11.2\%, 5 seeds) and Argmin-only (12.4\%, 5 seeds). Error bars: $\pm$1 std where available; hollow marker for $K{=}5$ ($n{=}1$).}
13	\label{fig:kvalue}
14	\end{figure}
15	
16	Performance increases monotonically with $K$: $K{=}1$ (10.3\%, 3 seeds) $\to$ $K{=}5$ (14.0\%, 1 seed) $\to$ $K{=}10$ (17.5\%, 6 seeds).
17	At $K{=}1$, CRM correction alone performs at the VLA-only level (11.2\%), confirming that the correction module requires multi-sample selection to be effective.
18	The monotonic trend parallels inference-time compute scaling in LLMs~\cite{snell2024scaling}, where repeated sampling with selection (best-of-$N$) improves output quality.
19	In our setting, more samples yield a better argmin starting point, which in turn enables more effective correction.
20	
21	The $K{=}5$ data point is preliminary ($n{=}1$), but it falls on the monotonic trend, suggesting that even moderate sample budgets can capture partial gains.
22	The compute cost scales linearly: $K{=}10$ requires 10 forward passes through the frozen VLA plus ${\sim}$0.3\,ms for CRM inference, a ${\sim}$10$\times$ increase in VLA compute.
23	
24	
25	\section{Applicability Boundary: Stochastic VLA Diversity}
26	\label{sec:applicability}
27	
28	The multi-sample pipeline requires stochastic action diversity across samples. We test this prerequisite by comparing SmolVLA (flow-matching, 450M) with OpenVLA~\cite{kim2024openvla} (autoregressive tokenization, 7B).
29

31	\centering

4	scope: global

6	  - workflow

8	  - writing

14	---
15	
16	# Step 5 — Iterative Refinement
17	
18	Step 4 全部章节落盘后，Paper Agent 进入 Step 5：把全文递交 Reviewer 评分，按反馈修改，直到收敛。
19	
20	**核心约束**：
21	- 串行——一轮 Reviewer 返回结果处理完才进下一轮，禁止并发请求
22	- 评分驱动——overall 提升才 accept 本轮，下降则 git revert
23	- 上限 5 轮——硬约束，达到上限即停，不论分数
24	
25	## 状态记录（git 是 truth source）
26	
27	每轮修改前 commit 当前论文目录，作为 revert 锚点。无需额外 state 文件。
28	
29	```
30	git -C projects/{project} add paper/
31	git -C projects/{project} commit -m "[paper] refinement round {N-1}"
32	```
33	
34	Round N 的 commit message 用 `N-1`，即 round 1 修改前 commit `round 0`（Step 4 落盘后的初稿）。
35	
36	## 单轮流程
37	
38	```
39	Round N（N = 1..5）：
40	
41	1. git commit 当前版本（message: "[paper] refinement round N-1"）
42	   └── 落地评分下降时的 revert 锚点
43	
44	2. 组装审查请求：
45	   - read_project_file("paper_checklist.yaml") — 用户自定义必查项
46	   - 拼 LaTeX 全文（或 Patch 模式下的差异）+ writing_plan.yaml 路径
47	   - 末尾追加 "## 用户自定义必查项"（见下方"checklist 注入格式"）
48	
49	3. paper_agent_request_review(text=组装后的请求, project=...)
50	   └── Reviewer 通过 reviewer_post_to_director 回传评分
51	
52	4. 解析 Reviewer 响应：
53	   - 维度分（Originality/Clarity/Soundness/Significance/Presentation，1-4）
54	   - overall（1-10）
55	   - 各条具体问题 severity（critical/warning/info）
56	   - checklist 项 PASS/FAIL
57	   ★ 任一 checklist FAIL → 该条问题 severity 自动按 critical 对待
58	
59	5. 按问题类型分流处理：
60	   - 事实性错误（数字不一致 / 引用错位）→ 自己用 edit_paper_section 改
61	   - 段落级叙事问题（逻辑断裂 / 强度词超限）→ spawn Worker 重写段落，回来再 edit_paper_section
62	   - 结构问题（章节顺序 / 段落归并）→ 自己用 edit_paper_section
63	   - critical 且 claim 本身无支撑 → 汇报 Director（不要自行降 claim）
64	
65	6. 全部问题处理完后判断本轮 overall 走势：
66	   - overall(N) ≥ overall(N-1) → 保留本轮修改，进入收敛判断
67	   - overall(N) <  overall(N-1) → git checkout 上一轮 commit，回退本轮所有修改，标记"本轮无进展"
68	
69	7. 收敛判断（任一满足即停）：
70

_source line 2181 · 2026-05-18T11:05:31.168640+00:00_

1	Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models for robotic manipulation degrade under distribution shift, yet improving their performance without retraining remains underexplored.
2	We propose a sample-select-correct pipeline for inference-time action refinement of frozen flow-matching VLAs that draws multiple action samples, selects the most representative candidate via $L_2$ argmin, and applies a learned residual correction bounded in magnitude.
3	The correction module is a ${\sim}$327K-parameter MLP conditioned on the frozen VLA's observation embedding, trained offline with supervised $L_2$ loss on demonstration data.
4	On the LIBERO-Object benchmark (10 tasks, 6 seeds), the pipeline achieves $17.5 \pm 2.8$\% versus $11.2 \pm 1.6$\% for the unmodified VLA (+6.3\,pp, $p = 0.0074$).
5	Component decomposition reveals that learned correction accounts for ${\sim}$5\,pp and sample selection for ${\sim}$1\,pp; neither component alone improves the baseline.
6	A systematic conditioning and magnitude study yields two findings: observation features produce robust corrections across all tested magnitude bounds (13.5--17.5\%), while per-DOF conformal uncertainty features cause progressive collapse (14\% to 0\%) as the bound increases.
7	We further show that multi-sample correction requires stochastic action diversity: tokenized VLAs produce near-identical samples, establishing an architectural applicability boundary.
8

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:intro}
3	
4	% --- Para 1: VLAs are impressive but fragile under distribution shift ---
5	Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) that generate actions via flow matching~\cite{black2024pi0,shukor2025smolvla,lipman2023flow} have reached near-saturation on in-distribution benchmarks such as LIBERO~\cite{liu2023libero}.
6	Yet this performance masks severe fragility: visual and physical perturbations common in deployment (camera viewpoint shifts, object layout changes, robot initial-state variation) routinely degrade success by over 65 percentage points.
7	On the LIBERO+ benchmark~\cite{fei2025liberoplus}, $\pi_0$ drops from 94.2\% to 6.6\% under initial-state perturbation, and UniVLA drops from 95.2\% to 4.3\% under viewpoint changes.
8	Closing this robustness gap without retraining the base policy is a prerequisite for deploying VLAs outside tightly controlled laboratory settings.
9	
10	% --- Para 2: Existing approaches and the gap ---
11	Two lines of work address this challenge.
12	Training-time methods improve the base policy through robustness-aware objectives~\cite{srpo2025,robustvla2025,digflow2025} or test-time reinforcement learning~\cite{liu2026ttvla}, but require retraining or per-task RL and remain bounded by the training distribution.
13	Inference-time methods leave the policy frozen: ReconVLA~\cite{chen2026reconvla} applies conformal quantile regression for uncertainty quantification and out-of-distribution detection, but does not learn corrective residuals; A2C2~\cite{a2c2_2025} trains a per-step correction head on frozen VLA action chunks, but applies correction unconditionally without multi-sample selection or explicit magnitude control; VLA-Pilot~\cite{li2025vlapilot} applies evolutionary optimization over action samples, but incurs 2.41\,s per control step.
14	Despite growing interest in inference-time action refinement, fundamental design questions remain unanswered: how much does selection contribute relative to cor
